INTRODUCTION
I, the Chairman of the Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Home
Affairs having been authorized by the Committee to present the Report on its behalf, do hereby
present this Ninety-second Report on the Companies (Amendment) Bill, 2001
*.
2. In pursuance of the rules relating to the Department-related Parliamentary Standing
Committees, the Chairman, Rajya Sabha in consultation with the Speaker, Lok Sabha referred ** the
Companies (Amendment) Bill, 2001, as introduced in the Lok Sabha on 30the August, 2001 and
pending therein, to the Committee on 7th September, 2001 for examination and report.
3.0. The Committee considered the Bill in nine sittings held on 31st October, 2001 and 6th and
20th February, 3rd, 4th, 5th and 19th June, 4th July and 17th July, 2002.
3.1 The Committee in its sitting held on 13th September, 2001 decided to issue a Press
Communique inviting suggestions/comments on the Bill from individuals/institutions/organisations
interested in the subject matter of the Bill within 15 days of its publication. The Press Communique
appeared in the leading newspapers on 24th September, 2001.
3.2 In its sitting held on 17th July, 2002, the Committee considered the draft Report on the Bill
and adopted the same.
3.3 In the sitting held on 17th July, 2005, the Committee also decided that the evidence
tendered before the Committee may be laid on the Table of both the Houses of Parliament.
4.0 The Committee also invited suggestions/views of the major Chambers of Commerce and
Industry, such as Confederation of Indian Industry (CII), Associated Chamber of Commerce and
Industry of India (ASSOCHAM), PHD Chamber of Commerce and Industry (PHDCCI), Federation
of Indian Chambers of Commerce and Industry (FICCI), Bombay Chamber of Commerce and
Industry (BCCI) and Indian Merchant Chamber (IMC), Professional bodies like, Institute of
Chartered Accountants of India (ICAI), Institute of Company Secretaries of India (ICSI) and
Institute of Cost and Works Accountants of India (ICWAI), Financial Institutions such as IBA, RBI,
SEBI, IDBI and ICICI, BIFR and major trade unions.
4.1 The Committee heard the views of the following organisations as per the schedule indicated
below:
Organisation/Association Date

5.0 In the course of its deliberations, the Committee has made use of the background note on
the Bill received from the Department of Company Affairs (Ministry of Law, Justice and Company
Affairs), replies of the Department of Company Affairs to the queries raised by the Members in
the sittings of the Committee and comments of Department of Company Affairs on memoranda
received from individuals/organisations. The list of individuals/organisations and gist of their
memoranda together with comments of Department of Company Affairs thereon is placed at
Annexure-V.
5.1 The Committee also relied on the following documents/papers while preparing the report:
(i) A comparative statement showing the amendments proposed in the Companies Bill,
1997, recommendations of the Committee on the Bill as contained in its sixty-fifth
Report on the Companies Bill, 1997, Provisions in the Companies (Amendment)
Bill, 2001 in respect of provisions relating to winding up/dissolution, insolvency of
companies and constitution of National Company Law Tribunal;
(ii) Companies Act as amended upto date;
(iii) Sick Industrial Companies (Special Provisions) Act, 1985;
(iv) Recovery of Debts due to Banks and Financial Institutions Act, 1993;
(v) Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956;
(vi) Insurance Act, 1938;
(vii) Industrial (Development and Regulation) Act, 1951; and
(viii)  Report of the High Level Committee on Law relating to insolvency and winding up
of companies (2000).
6. For facility of reference and convenience, observations and recommendations of the
Committee have been printed in bold letters in the body of the Report.

1
REPORT
The Companies (Amendment) Bill, 2001 (Annexure–I)  proposes to put in place the National
Company Law Tribunal (hereinafter referred to as NCLT) and the National Company Law Appellate
Tribunal (hereinafter referred to as NCLAT) so as to take over functions being hitherto performed
by the Company Law Board, the Board for Industrial and Financial Reconstruction (BIFR)
1 and the
Appellate Authority for Industrial and Financial Reconstruction (AAIFR) 2 as well as by High Courts/
District Courts in winding up of Companies.
1.1 The Bill, therefore, seeks to amend relevant Sections/Parts/Schedule of the Companies Act,
1956 (hereinafter referred to as Principal Act), with a view to expediting revival/rehabilitation of sick
companies and protection of workers’ interests and where necessary winding up of companies.
2. The Bill intends to integrate the extant procedure relating to restructuring and winding up
of companies. Currently, revival and rehabilitation of sick companies are being administered under
the Sick Industrial Companies (Special Provisions) Act, 1985 (SICA) by the BIFR whereas winding
up/liquidation of the insolvent companies rest with the High Courts/District Courts under the
Principal Act. Winding up of companies is proposed by the BIFR as a last resort in case revival
and rehabilitation measures fail to take off.
3. The proposals contained in the Bill are basically based on the recommendations contained
in the Report of the High Level Committee
3 on law relating to insolvency and winding up of
companies (2000).
4.0 The High Level Committee has stated that BIFR and AAIFR have not been able to fulfil the
purpose and mandate as envisaged under SICA for providing viable scheme for the revival of sick
companies in a reasonable short time frame. The status of cases registered with BIFR as on
30th June, 2000, as submitted to the Eradi Committee, is produced in the accompanying Table.
TABLE-I
Status of cases registered with BIFR as on 30.6.2000

1 BIFR and AAIFR which were set up under the Sick Industrial Companies (Special Provisions) Act, 1985 (SICA)
are proposed to be dissolved upon the enactment of the Companies (Amendment) Bill, 2001 which would be done
through the repeal of Sick Industrial Companies (Special provisions) Act, 1985. The Sick Industrial Companies
(Special Provisions) Repeal Bill, 2001 has been introduced in Lok Sabha on 30 August, 2001 (on the same day the
Companies (Amendment) Bill, 2001 was also introduced and referred to the Standing Committee on Finance for
consideration and report.
2 ibid.
3 The Committee is a 10 Member body comprising nine experts under the Chairmanship of Justice V . Balakrishna
Eradi. It was constituted on 22nd October, 1999 to examine laws relating to insolvency and winding up of
companies and it submitted its report on 31st July, 2002. The Committee examined Companies Act, 1956 and other
laws having bearing on the subject which inter alia  include Sick Industrial Companies (Special Provisions) Act, 1985
(SICA), Recovery of Debts due to Banks and Financial Institutions Act, 1993 and Securities Contract (Regulation)
Act, 1956.

4.1 The said Committee has also noted that there is substantial time lag between the date of
presentation of the petition for winding up and the date of winding up order by the High Court 4.
4.2 There is abnormal delay in the dissolution of companies because of long drawn Court
proceedings. The proceeding is long drawn due to non-submission of statement of affairs and
production of books of accounts and records and assets of the Company by the ex-director of the
Company as a result of which the Official Liquidator (OL), files proceedings under sections 454, 456
and 468 for non-submission of aforesaid documents which led to multiplicity of Court proceedings.
4.3 The age-wise pendency of Companies under compulsory winding up under the charge of
OL as on 31.12.99, as submitted to the Eradi Committee is presented in the accompanying Table.

4 High Court has exclusive jurisdiction to deal with winding up of companies. There is a Company Court with
Company Judge in each High Court, who periodically rotate within 6 months. An Officer belonging to Indian
Company Law Service designated Official Liquidator (OL) is appointed by the Department of Company Affairs and
attached to every High Court who acts as liquidator of a Company to be wound up by that High Court. The OL
also works under the supervision, control and direction of the Court. The OL is also party to the case who cannot
move the Court for dissolution of the sick company unless all pending cases relating to winding up of Company are
finally disposed off.

5. The Committee in its Sixty-fifth Report on Companies Bill, 1997 (para 21) had taken note
of the setting up of the Eradi Committee on the laws relating to insolvency. Some of the
recommendations of the Committee on clause 416 of the 1997 Bill (para 23.54) have been accepted
and provided in clause 51 of the present Bill which are as follows:
• Representation of professionals, academics and others from a wide cross-section of
the society may be considered for the proposed Tribunal.
• Professional liquidators may be appointed from a panel of Chartered Accountants,
Advocates, Company Secretaries, Cost and Works Accountants etc.
6. The Ministry has submitted the following reasons in support of the need for changes in
Insolvency law:
• Winding up and adjudication of insolvency of companies takes a very long time over
20-25 years.
• Multiplicity of litigation before various Courts/Quasi Judicial bodies/forums be avoided.
• The productive resources of the unit remain blocked and unutilized.
• Assets left stripped; inordinate delay results in assets losing value.
• Present Insolvency law not in tune with the international practices like USA, Australia,
New Zealand, Singapore etc.
• Present system is a Zero sum game.
• BIFR has not served the purpose for which it was set up for preventive, ameliorative
and reconstructionist measures for sick companies.
7.0 One Hundred thirty-five Sections (from 425 to 559) under Part VII divided into five
chapters of the Principal Act deals with winding up of companies. The Principal Act enumerates
the following three modes of winding up of Companies:—
(i) Compulsory winding up (Winding up by the Court) – Chapter II– Sections 433-483
(ii) V oluntary winding up (by creditors and Members) Chapter III, Section 484-521
(iii) W inding up subject to supervision of Court – Chapter IV , Sections 522 to 527
7.1 The winding up procedure by the court takes a long time in view of multiple proceedings
in the court relating to various facets of sick companies. There is no time-frame prescribed for
the High Court as it would tantamount to interference in the independence and integrity of
judiciary.

4
7.2 Though a time-frame has been provided for BIFR under SICA for rehabilitation and revival
of sick industrial companies, it has been hardly adhered to. Furthermore, the orders of BIFR have
also been challenged in the court of law. Both the factors combined makes the revival procedure
of sick companies a long drawn affair.
8.0 The salient features of the Companies (Amendment) Bill, 2001are outlined below:
8.1 National Company Law Tribunal (NCLT)
• A National Company Law Tribunal be set up : (Section 10FB). The NCLT would be
an alternative mechanism to deal with the problem of sickness, revival and
rehabilitation as well as winding up of insolvent Companies;
• The Tribunal shall consist of President, Judicial and Technical Members;
• The President of Tribunal will be sitting or retired Judge of a High Court; There shall
be upto 62 members in the Tribunal;
• There shall be single member and two member benches to deal with the cases as
determined by the President of the Tribunal;
• There shall be 10 Special Benches each having a least three members to look after the
revival, rehabilitation and winding up of companies; and
• There shall be one member on all Special Benches having knowledge and experience
of labour laws.
8.2 The Power and Jurisdiction of the Tribunal:
– Power and jurisdiction presently exercised by the Company Law Board will be
transferred to the Tribunal such as:
Powers of Regional Benches:
– Change of Registered Office of the Company – sections 17, 18 and 19.
– Default in repayment of deposits by companies – sections 58A, 58AA.
– Redemption of irredeemable preference shares – section 80A.
– Appeals against refusal of registration of transfer of shares and debentures by the
company – section 111;
– Extension of time for delivery of certificates – section 113
– Rectification of register of charges and extension of time for the registration of a
charge – section 141;
– Enforce duty of company to make returns etc. – section 614.
Powers of Principal Bench
– to order investigation of the affairs of a company – sections 235, 237 and 247
– imposition of restrictions upon shares and debentures and prohibition of transfer of
shares and debentures – section 250
– inquire cases against managerial personnel – section 388 B
– issue order for relief in cases of oppression – section 402

5
– order effective safeguard of the interests of company – section 408
– prevent change in Board of Directors likely to affect company prejudicially –
section 409
– order an immediate inspection of the registers and returns – section 163;
– call Annual General Meeting if company fails to call the same – section 167;
– order immediate inspection of the minute book etc. – section 196;
– prevention of oppression and mismanagement – existing provisions of the Act, sections
397 and 398 etc.
8.3 Other Powers and Jurisdiction transferred to the Tribunal
• to consider rehabilitation and revival of companies – a mandate presently entrusted to
BIFR/AAIFR under SICA will be transferred to the Tribunal;
• Winding up, merger and amalgamation of companies presently vested in High Courts
will be transferred to the Tribunal.
8.4 Reference to be made to the Tribunal on sickness of a company by: (Sec. 424A)
• Board of Directors of a Company;
• Central/State Government – in the case of Government companies;
• RBI/Financial Institution.
8.5 Basis for making reference to the Tribunal:
• Debt default or erosion of 50% or more of average net worth during four years
immediately preceding financial year shall be the basis of reference.
8.6 National Company Law Appellate Tribunal (NCLAT):
• The Appellate Tribunal shall have a Chairperson and two members.
• The Chairperson of the Appellate Tribunal shall be sitting or retired judge of Supreme
Court or Chief Justice of High Court.
• Any person aggrieved by the order or decisions of the Tribunal may prefer an appeal
to the Appellate Tribunal.
• Appellate Tribunal shall have contempt power.
• An appeal against the order of Appellate Tribunal will lie before Supreme Court only
on point of law.
8.7 Time Schedule provided in the Bill:
– Reference to Tribunal on Sickness of companies – 180 days—Section 424A(4).
– Tribunal to pass orders – 60 days—Section 424B(4).
– Special Directors to give report to Tribunal – 60 days—Section 424B(6).
– Operating agency to prepare a scheme – 60 days—Section 424D.

6
– Scheme for Financial revival–60 days—Section 424E(2).
– Winding up of a company after passing order for winding up – one year—Section 424
G(1) and 424G(4).
Whole exercise will be completed within a span of 730 days to 845 days.
8.8 Winding up of Companies:
• In case it appears to the Tribunal that revival/reconstruction is not possible it will
order for winding up of companies. (Bill Sec.424G)
• There shall be professional Liquidators in addition to the Official Liquidator. A panel in
this regard to be maintained by the Central Government (Clause 51).
• Tribunal shall deal all the cases of winding up of companies except companies
registered under Banking (Regulation) Act, 1948 (Clause 122).
8.9 Rehabilitation and Revival Fund (Clause 45)
• There shall be levied and collected cess @ 0.005% (maximum limit fixed 0.1%) on the
annual turnover of companies. (Therefore, Rs. 5/- only will be collected from a
company on its annual turnover of Rs.l lakh).
• Cess to be fixed for:
– making interim payment of workmen’s dues pending the revival or rehabilitation
of the sick industrial company; and
– payment of workmen’s dues due to the workmen of the sick industrial company,
or
– protection of assets of sick industrial company, or
– revival or rehabilitation of sick industrial company.
8.10 Novel Features:
• For the first time, time bound disposal of revival, rehabilitation and winding up of
companies has been provided;
• Contempt power has been given to Appellate Tribunal;
• Multiplicity of litigation before various Courts/Quasi judicial bodies/forums has been
avoided;
• The concept of cess to help workmen and revival of sick companies has been
introduced.
8.11 The Secretary, Department of Company Affairs in his presentation on the Bill, has
mentioned the following advantages in enacting the Bill:
• It will reduce the entire process which at present can even last for about 20 to 25
years in winding up of the companies to about two years or so;
• Stripping of assets of sick companies will be avoided;
• Multiplicity of litigation before various Courts/Quasi judicial bodies/forums will also be
avoided and be put to an end as all these matters will be heard and decided by the
proposed National Company Law Tribunal;

7
• All the parties will be bound by the National Tribunal’s orders and in case of non-
availability of workable proposal for revival/rehabilitation etc. the National Tribunal may
decide the matter on merits;
• All cases of revival/rehabilitation/reconstruction/arrangement and winding up of
companies will be under one roof;
• Efforts of the National Company Law Tribunal will be to revive/rehabilitate the
companies and as a last resort to order winding up; and
• All cases of revival/rehabilitation/reconstruction/arrangement and winding up of
companies will be under one roof.
9.0 The Bill in total has 122 clauses and one schedule. Around one hundred and eleven clauses
(Clauses 6 to 34, 36 to 44, 46 to 45, 52 to 54 and 56 to 121) relate to the matters relating to
NCLT/NCALT. One clause i.e. clause 6 inter alia deals with setting up, composition, power, etc.
of NCLT and NCALT whereas other one hundred and ten clauses are consequential amendments
so as to transfer powers presently rested with CLB/High Courts to NCLT. Clause 6 seeks to insert
thirty-four sections (l0FB, 10FC, 10FD, 10EF, 10FF, 10FG, 10FH, 10FI, 10FJ, l0FK, 10FL, l0FM,
l0FN, l0FO, 10FP, 10FQ, 10FR, 10FS, 10FT, 10FU, 10FV , 10FW, 10FX, 10FY , l0FZ, 10FZA, l0G,
10GA, l0GAA, 10GB, 10GC, 10GD, l0GE and 10GF) under Part IB after part IA of the Principal
Act. Clauses 7 to 34 seeks to amend Sections 17, 18, 19, 43, 49, 55A, 58A, 58AA, 75, 79, 80A,
104, 111, l1lA, 113, 117B, 117C, 118, 141, 144, 163 167,168,186, 188, 196, 203, 219, 225, 235,
236, 237, 241, 243, 247, 250, 251, 269, 284, 304, 307, 318, 391, 392, 394A, 395, 396 and 410
of the Principal Act. Clauses 36 to 44 seeks to amend Sections 425, to 427,433 to 438, 440 and
441, clauses 52 to 54 amends Sections 450, 451, 453 to 456 and 457 and clause 56 to 121 amends
Sections 458, 458A, 459 to 481, 490, 492, 494, 497, 502, 503, 504, 506, 507, 509, 511A, 512,
513, 515, 517, 518, 519, 522 to 527, 531, 531A, 533, 535, 536 to 538, 540, 542, 543 to 551,
553 to 560, 581, 582, 587 to 589, 610, 614, 621A, 626, 627, 632, 635, 635B, 637A, 640A, 643
and 647 A and schedule XI of the Principal Act.
9.1 The rest ten clauses including clause one which is about short title and year of
commencement, inter  alia deals with dissolution of CLB, rehabilitation and revival of sick
companies, Rehabilitation and Revival Fund, appointment of private liquidators, etc.
9.2 Clause 2 proposes to amend Section 2 of the Principal Act, to insert certain new definitions
therein i.e. sick industry
5, industrial company, industrial undertaking, net worth, etc. besides
substituting certain words in view of proposed transfer of power of CLB/High Court to NCLT.
Clauses 3 and 4 are consequential in nature and amends l0E and 10F of the principal Act,
respectively which spells out that CLB/HC would be discharging their function till the
commencement of this Act.
9.3 Clause 5 deals with matters relating to dissolution of CLB.
9.4 Clause 35 provides for insertion of new Part VIA in the Principal Act, consisting of new
sections 424A, 424B, 424C, 424D, 424E, 424F, 424G, 424H, 4241, 424J, 424K and 424L relating
to revival, rehabilitation and winding up of sick industrial companies.
5 The definition of sick company is slightly different from that laid down in the SICA. SICA deals about two types
of sick companies viz. (i) sick company where cent per cent erosion of net worth is reported/found (ii) potential
sick company where net worth erosion is fifty per cent or more but less than cent per cent. The revised definition
as proposed in the Bill include those companies wherein fifty per cent erosion of average net worth during the
preceding four years or debt default within any three consecutive quarters. Thus the concept of potential sick
company would be done away with the enactment of the proposed legislation.

8
9.5 Clause 45 seeks to insert new Sections 441A, 44IB, 441C, 441D, 441E and 441F in the
Principal Act for formation of Rehabilitation and Revival Fund (RRF) by way of levy of cess not
less than 0.005% and not more than 0.1% on the value of annual turnover of every company or
on its gross receipt, whichever is more.
9.6 Clause 50 prescribes punishment for director or concerned officers of the company which
is to be wound up by the NCLT in case of failure on their part in submission of up-to-date audited
book of account of the company for which a new section 446A is proposed in the Principal Act.
9.7 Clause 51 substitute Section 448 of the Principal Act in order to make provision of
appointment of private liquidator Clause 55 is consequential in nature.
9.8 Clause 122 is a consequential clause which inserts a new section 651A for making provision
to transfer all cases of winding up pending in various Courts under various Acts except Banking
Regulation Act, to the Tribunal.
10. Before the Committee could take up the Bill clause-by-clause, some fundamental issues
concerning the Bill were raised by members which are given below along with the comments of
the Ministry:
Rationality of creating NCLT
11.0 A section of members in the Committee felt that instead of repealing SICA, 1985
and replacing BIFR with NCLT. BIFR should be provided with more teeth as cases pending
with it would be abated leading to serious socio-economic problems since 1306 cases relating
to sick companies are pending at various stages in BIFR and AAFIR involving 15 lakh
employees/workers. Furthermore, the aim and objective of the present Bill is different from
that of SICA. In this context, the Committee wanted to know from the Government the
rationale behind creation of NCLT. The Committee was not convinced with the
Government’s explanation that BIFR/AAIFR was not effective.
11.1 The Government responded by stating that corporate restructuring, including insolvency and
winding up, and dispute resolution, for the Indian corporates was currently handled in three
different quasi-judicial/judicial forums, namely, the Company Law Board, the BIFR and the High
Courts. There was a need to provide a single window service to the Indian corporates. Hence the
proposal to have one body, the NCLT, and to wind up both the BIFR and the CLB.
11.2 The Government further stated that referring all matters to one single body will have the
advantage of that body being able to give focussed attention to company matters; the body
becoming a repository of expertise in the area of company law, and the efficiency the resulting
synergy would impart on the single body.
11.3 The Government stated that it may also be pointed out that the Justice Eradi Committee,
and a Group of Ministers had deliberated at length on these issues, including the possibility of only
amending SICA. Eradi Committee had specifically noted the advantage of having a single forum
instead of multiple fora. As would be clear from the above, mere amendment of SICA would not
have the multiple advantages which seems inherent in creating a single forum.
11.4 It was also stated by Government that various provisions have been introduced in the Bill
to bring about basic/structural improvements in the law which will facilitate more professional and
speedier finalisation of cases e.g. time-limits, binding nature of the Tribunal’s orders, and private
liquidators.
11.5 A point was raised that the desirability of concentrating and vesting such large and divergent
powers in one single body needed to be re-examined. It was felt that such concentration of powers

9
in one single forum was likely to result in further delays in concluding the liquidation proceedings.
A lot of time would be taken up in shaping its infrastructure and organisational structure and in
transfer of cases from CLB and different High Courts to NCLT. Giving a specific example it was
said that it took as many as 3 to 4 years in transfer of cases from High Courts to the Debt
Recovery Tribunals (DRTs) and in the meantime, the proceedings remained unattended. It was felt
that the combined task ranging from revival to dissolution of companies that was sought to be
entrusted to NCLT, would be beyond the physical capacity of anyone forum even it was divided
among different branches. Further, the winding up jurisdiction of High Courts was geographically
spread out and hence nearer to the parties concerned. Abolition of that may deprive companies,
creditors and workers of the advantages of geographical proximity.
11.6 Commenting on the overburdening of NCLT by transferring powers of administrative nature
to it, the Committee felt that administrative functions should not be vested in the NCLT, rather it
should remain with the administrative Department.
11.7 The Government informed the Committee that under the proposed dispensation NCLT was to
be empowered by transferring powers presently being exercised by High Courts in respect of
corporate restructuring and winding up, and transferring powers exercised by AAIFR/BIFR in regard
to rehabilitation and restructuring under SICA. In addition, powers for grievance redressal and dispute
resolution presently being handled by CLB are also to be transferred to the proposed NCLT.
11.8 The Government agreed with the views of the Committee that the NCLT, which was a high
level body, should not be burdened with routine functions presently handled by CLB. Besides, there
were certain issues relating to company matters which needed very speedy responses and timely
action to prevent or control developments that are detrimental to the interests of the share-holders
or the company. In addition, there were some issues which were purely administrative or routine in
nature requiring timely clearances. DCA further agreed that the proposed NCLT need not be
burdened with such issues and there was a clear advantage in transferring powers relating to such
issues to the DCA rather than the NCLT. An illustrative list of such provisions that have been
identified is given at Annexure-II.
11.9 The Committee noted the responses of the Ministry to the various issues raised.
However, a section of Members was not satisfied with the stand taken by the Ministry and
reiterated that status-quo in regard to Sick Industrial Companies Act (SICA) may be
maintained and BIFR/AAFIR may be strengthened by plugging the loopholes. However,
provisions may be made in the present Bill to transfer cases to NCLT for winding up only
from BIFR/AAIFR.
Rule Making Powers
12.0 The Committee wondered as to who would make the rules for NCLT, as in the case
of High Courts, Supreme Court made the rules.
12.1 The Department informed the Committee that liquidation and winding up proceedings were
being transferred from the High Courts to the NCLT. When they were with the High Courts, the
Companies (Court) Rules were prescribed by the Supreme Court, perhaps because rules had to be
common across all the High Courts to ensure harmonious functioning. Since this logic would no
longer hold good after the formation of the NCLT, the Rules for the NCLT could be prescribed
by the Central Government, as suggested by the Committee, in consultation with NCLT. There was
perhaps no need to waste the precious time of the Supreme Court on this small matter.
12.2 It was further stated by the Government that it was also essential that the rules should
facilitate quick decision making and provide for summary procedures in contrast to the lengthy
judicial procedures, on account of which cases often take years to be decided in the courts.

10
12.3 The Committee while looking at the structure of the NCLT felt that it was becoming
overtly judicial. It was of the view that there should be representatives from all walks of
life in the Tribunal, viz. economics, law, labour interests etc.
12.4 The Government agreed with the Committee that various provisions of the Bill, as they
stood today, would tend to overload this body with too many judicial officers, with the consequence
that the approach of NCLT to matters before it could become very legalistic and the process and
procedures might become as time consuming as they were in the Courts. There was also a need to
have greater involvement of experts, such as economists, bankers, accountants etc. on this body,
which would deal with what was essentially a socio-economic legislation requiring quick decisions
and summary procedures. In the light of the Committee’ s observations, the Government has agreed
to have a relook at proposed Sections 10 FL (6) and 10 FR (3).
Imposition of Cess for Rehabilitation and Revival Fund
13.0 A point was raised about the constitutional validity of the Cess. An opinion was
expressed that if the Cess collected went to the Consolidated Fund of India (CFI), it would
be difficult to retrieve that money for rehabilitation of sick units. It felt that it should be
separated from it. The Department in its reply said that the Bill had been thoroughly
examined by the Legal and Legislative Departments. The constitutional validity of the cess
did not therefore, seem to be in doubt.
13.1 The Department further stated that, in so far as the Committee’s suggestion regarding
separation of the cess from the CFI was concerned, the CFI was normally not divisible. If,
however, there was a decision/direction to this ef fect, then a separate legislation along the lines of
The Central Road Fund Act, 2000 would have to be brought. It was perhaps not possible to include
this in the scope of the current Bill.
13.2 A point was raised that the levy of cess shall be upon all companies. It was suggested
that Section 441A (Part VIA of the Bill) should restrict itself in its application to “industrial
companies” as defined under the section 2 (f) of the Bill for the following reason:
The “Rehabilitation and Revival Fund” will not be available for the rehabilitation
or revival of Private Financial Institutions (PFIs), Banks, or Service Sector. It,
therefore, would not be equitable to expect contributions from these bodies
towards the fund since it had no application and therefore no relevance to them.
13.3 The Committee felt that there was a need to cover the Service Sector under the
Revival and Rehabilitation Scheme, because the Government was levying cess on them. It
felt that it would not be a fair deal, if they are asked to contribute to the Revival and
Rehabilitation Fund without incentives given to them.
13.4 In response the Government stated that service companies generally are not assets-based,
in that they do not have adequate tangible/physical assets unlike industrial companies which are
covered in the Bill. (There are of course exceptions in the case of service companies which do
have tangible assets, like hotels). In the absence of any tangible assets, the scope for revival from
out of the accretions or appreciation in the value of these assets appears remote. Inclusion of
service companies can also increase several times the number of rehabilitation cases that the NCLT
may have to deal with. It may be preferable at least initially to confine the jurisdiction to
industrial companies only. After the NCLT is well established and sufficient experience has been
gained it can be considered whether the scope be widened to include service companies.
Institutionalising early warning systems to avert sickness
14.0 The Committee was strongly in favour of devising a mechanism to detect sickness
of industries at an early stage.

11
14.1 The Department in its response said that the revised definition of ‘sickness’ in the Bill was
itself an early warning signal compared with the definition in SICA because:—
(i) In the bill, erosion of even 50% of net worth would be classified as sickness instead
of 100% as in SICA; and
(ii) Default in payment of debt over three quarters would also be classified as sickness,
whereas in the SICA, there was no criteria relating to default in payment of debt.
14.2 It further said that, these two criteria in the new definition of ‘Sickness’ would enable early
information about sickness and much more timely measures for rehabilitation compared with the
present definition in SICA.
14.3 The Committee felt that financial institutions/Banks were always in a better position
to detect sickness. Therefore, it should be made obligatory on them to inform the NCLT,
as and when they notice the sickness.
14.4 The Ministry replied that in view of their above explanations, it may not be necessary to
include a further warning system because this may burden the NCLT with deluge of letters.
However, if the Co mmittee so felt, some provisions could be introduced in the Bill putting
responsibility on the companies or the financial institutions to detect signs of potential/approaching
sickness and merely send an intimation to NCLT along with description of the steps being taken
by the company/institutions to prevent further deterioration in the company towards sickness. A
broad provision could be introduced in the bill allowing details to be handled through rule making
powers e.g., measures such as the following can be considered for inclusion in the rules:—
(i) In case of default in payment of large-sized debts, the company itself could be charged
with the responsibility of reporting to the NCLT the first default itself. A Group of (at
least five) creditors could also be allowed to report such a first default.
(ii) In other cases, banks/financial institutions could be charged with the responsibility of
reporting to the NCLT a case of potential sickness (even if net worth had not eroded to
the extent of 50%) if it appeared to them from the returns and reports filed by the company
with them, or from the knowledge/papers available with their nominee director, if any, that
the company was on the road to sickness. The banks/financial institutions could also be
required to indicate the steps they are taking to improve matters.
14.5 The Department informed the Committee that if such a provision was made, the law will
also have to provide for the action that the NCLT might take in such cases. NCLT could then be
empowered to inform such stake holders or give such directions to those concerned as it thinks
necessary to prevent the foreseeable sickness.
Timely disposal of cases
15.0 A point was raised that in the BIFR, the number of days taken for sanction of a
scheme was 270 days, whereas in the case of the NCLT, it was 580 days. The Committee
wanted to know from the Department of Company Affairs as to how it (the Department)
was planning to expedite things when the time limit was more in the new regime.
15.1 The Department informed the Committee that in fact some of the time limits set in SICA
were indicative rather than mandatory. For example, in sub-section (3) of Section 16 of the Act,
the BIFR was required to only endeavor to keep the time limit of 60 days. In respect of some
activities, time limits were altogether open ended. For example, in clause (a) of sub-section (3) of
Section 18, comments on a scheme are to be invited by the Board “within such period as the Board
may specify”.

12
15.2 Moreover the Department wanted to know as to how 270 days have been calculated.
According to BIFR itself, as reported in the Eradi Committee, time taken could be as much as 22
months, or 660 days. These calculations are reproduced at Annexure III. Time frame prescribed
in the new Bill is shorter, as Annexure IV  would show.
15.3 The Department further stated that in any case it had never been the Government’s case
that SICA was being repealed only because of delays, or that the only improvement in the proposed
legislation was the possible quicker disposal of cases. As stated earlier, the primary advantage of
the proposed legislation was the synergy that it would ensure vis-a-vis the present system where
corporates had to appear before three different forums and the improvements made in the law.
Definition of sick industry/undertaking
15.4 Another point was raised that for the purpose of rehabilitation/revival of undertakings, both
the Public Sector Undertakings and Private Sector Undertakings have been clubbed together. This
can be illustrated as follows:
(i) The definition of “Sick Units” in the Bill does not define Public Sector Undertakings
and Private Sector Undertakings separately and they are treated at par. Separate
provisions should be there in the Bill for them.
(ii) A Public Sector Undertaking if and when it becomes a sick unit, the blame directly
or indirectly falls on Government. They are answerable to Government and Parliament
whereas a Private Sector Undertaking is answerable to shareholders and financial
institutions.
(iii) An undertaking is deemed to be a sick unit when accumulated losses are equal to 50%
or more of its average net worth in anyone or more of four immediate preceding
financial years, or which failed to repay its debt within any three consecutive quarters
on demand for its repayment by the creditors of such Company (Clause 46AA).
(iv) But in case of Public Sector Undertakings Clause 46AA should only apply to trade
debts. It neither applies to loans, subsidies advanced by the Government who are the
owners nor to money advanced by their financial agencies. Private Sector Undertakings
are profit making ventures while many of the Public Sector Undertakings were set up
on socio-economic considerations.
(v) Cases are there where ventures were taken up not to make profit, but to make finished
products available to the people at an affordable price. Consequently Government gave
advances, subsidies and loans, primarily to make up the expected losses. Now due to
mismanagement, siphoning of funds, incompetent officers and financial crunch the
Government wants to discontinue running of the PSUs. The step to be adopted is to
declare them as sick unit. Making the PSU a sick unit with the idea of winding them
up neither meets the socio-economic objective, nor meets the goal of giving every
unemployed citizen employment.
(vi) The revival provision of the Bill has to be positive not cosmetic. Emphasis on revival
and not liquidation has to be the first objective. The sick Public Sector Undertakings
should be allowed to function with financial aid for some time and after having judged
its performance, efforts be made for revival and liquidation should be the last resort.
Government’s comments
15.5.0 The Companies Act, 1956 defines ‘Private Companies’ in section 3 and ‘Government

13
Companies (PSU)’ in section 617. In Clause 35 of the Companies (Amendment) Bill, 2001,
proposed section 424A clearly provides that the Government Companies are not covered by this
provision where compulsory referencing to the Tribunal is provided for erosion of the net worth
of a company or for its default in making payment of debt. However, the Government Companies
may also make references to the Tribunal with the prior approval of the Government. Once they
are before the tribunal all companies are, and should be, equal.
15.5.1 Further, the Government clarified that there is a difference at reference stage. It may not
be sustainable to legally differentiate between Government and other companies once they are
before the Tribunal. Of course, since each case is to be decided by high-level tribunal, it is
expected that all attendant circumstances will be taken into account.
15.5.2 Further, the Government stated that the intention of this Legislation is to revive and
rehabilitate sick companies. Winding up of companies is the last option to be exercised.
16.0 Out of the 122 clauses in the Bill, the clauses discussed in the succeeding paras are
those wherein either the Committee or the Ministry has submitted/recommended certain
views/amendments. All other clauses including the Schedule have been adopted by the
Committee without any change.
Clause 2
16.1 This clause seeks to amend section 2 of the Companies Act, 1956 which relates to
definitions.
Suggestion
(i) The term ‘demand’ should be amplified.
(ii) The definition of ‘Net worth’ does not consider practical situations. There may be cases
where ‘Notes to accounts’ refer to certain non-provisions/undercharging of expenses etc.
Auditors may have also qualified their reports for such non-provisions/undercharge
expenses. Total effect of the same on paid up capital and free reserves be taken into
account for determining the net worth. The same may result in eroding fifty per cent or
entire net worth of the company.
Government’s views in seriatim
(i) Suggestion accepted and could be covered in the rules.
(ii) Suggestion agreed to. However, to deal with the notes to accounts for non-charging and
undercharging of expenses, rules could be made.
Committee’s recommendation
16.2 The Committee recommends that the clause may be suitably amended so as to
incorporate the suggestions accepted by the Government.
16.3 Subject to above the clause is adopted.
Clause 5
16.4 This clause seeks to insert new section 10FA in the Companies Act, 1956 to provide for
dissolution of the Company Law Board and the National Company Law Tribunal was proposed to
be constituted under the Act. The Chairman, Vice-Chairman and Members of the CLB would cease

14
to function from the date the NCLT was constituted without any compensation being given.
However, all matters or proceedings or cases pending before the CLB on or before the constitution
of the NCLT would be transferred to the NCLT.
16.5 The Committee is of the view that the Bill should provide for continuation of the
services of the Chairman, Vice-Chairman, Members and other employees in the Tribunal for
the following reasons:
(i) Unlike other bodies like BIFR/MRTP wherein members are appointed on tenure
basis, in the CLB members have been appointed on regular basis as in the case
of ITAT/CEGAT and as such their services should not be terminated.
(ii) Unlike the matters pending before BIFR which would abate on establishment of
the Tribunal, the matters pending before the CLB would be transferred to the
Tribunal and therefore to ensure continuity, the services of the Members should
be protected. Therefore, the Government could not take the stand that since the
services of the Members of BIFR were sought to be terminated, the same was
applicable to the members of the CLB.
(iii) It is to be noted that in the certain Bills amending the Companies Act, 1956,
presented before the Parliament earlier, statutory protection had been provided
for the Members of the CLB. It was also to be noted that the Companies Bill,
1997, containing the provisions for statutory protection to the Members in
Clause 416 (2) had been cleared by the Committee.
(iv) Fur ther, the present Bill had no provisions for a Vice President in the NCLT. All
Tribunals in India which had more than one regional bench had Vice Presidents
viz. ITAT, CEGAT, CAT, Settlement Commissions etc. Therefore, in the NCLT
also there should be atleast one Vice President.
(v) In view of the above, Clause 5 (2) should be amended on the following lines:
“Provided that the Central Government, by notification, continue the
appointment of the Chairman, Vice-Chairman or any other Member of the
Board of Company Law Administration, functioning as such before the
commencement of this Act, as President, Vice President or any other member
of the Tribunal/appellate Tribunal for such period not less than their existing
terms of office as may be specified in the notification.”
(vi) Most of the staff of the CLB are those who came on deputation and got
absorbed in the CLB. Since all the matters before CLB are to be transferred to
the Tribunal, all the staff of the CLB should also be taken on the Tribunal and
consequent provision should be made in the Bill.
Committee’s recommendation
16.6 The Committee recommends that suitable amendments may be made in clause 5 in
order to incorporate the above suggestions.
16.7 Subject to the above observations and suggested amendments the clause is adopted.
Clause 6
16.8 This clause relates to empowering the Tribunal to give direction regarding the disposal of
assets.

15
Suggestion
Section 424I empowers the Tribunal to give direction regarding the disposal of the assets
during the preparation or consideration of the scheme under section 424C. It is felt that such an
order should be passed at a stage anterior to the preparation of the scheme i.e. during the period
of inquiry under section 424B.
Government’s views
Suggestion accepted.
Committee’s recommendation
16.9 The clause may be suitably amended to incorporate the suggestion accepted by the
Government.
16.10 Subject to above, the clause is adopted.
Clause 35
16.11 This clause provides for insertion of new Part VIA in the Companies Act, 1956 relating to
revival, rehabilitation and winding up of sick industrial companies.
Suggestions
(i) The Special Director is required to submit a report to the Tribunal within 60 days form the
commencement of the enquiry about the state of affairs of the company. There are divergent views
as to when the enquiry commences viz. on the date of making a reference to the Tribunal or the
date of first hearing of the reference. Therefore, instead of the words “from the commencement
of the enquiry”, the report may be called for “within 60 days from the date of his appointment.”
(ii) The proposed section gives the powers to the Tribunal to appoint special directors on the
Board of sick industrial companies. However, the Bill is silent about remunerating such directors.
The requisite amendments be made to the Bill.
(iii) The section should enable the workers/employees to make a reference to the Tribunal, either
on their own or through his/her union representative at the plant/unit level, state level or Central
level.
(iv) For filing of reference within 180 days extendable by a further period of 90 days, totaling
to 270 days which is almost equivalent to a year. As usual, if the company extends its financial
year as per permissible provisions the period would be too long when the case of sickness would
come up for consideration with the Tribunal.
(v) It is also an impractical time frame that the operating agency is required to complete its
enquiry within 21 days from the date of the order, which period is extendable by 40 days.
(vi) The Bill has not defined the term ‘2/3rd creditors’ with the result that interpretational
difficulties will arise. Some may interpret it as 2/3rd creditors in terms of numbers and some
others may define it as in terms of value. If it is to be taken as 2/3 creditors in terms of numbers
this would lead to a very difficult situation in that a sick company might have thousands of
creditors in the form of FIs/Banks/Fixed Deposit holders/Debenture holders etc. resulting in
considerable delays in getting the necessary 2/3 concurrence. If it is defined in terms of value,
working capital Banks may be at a serious disadvantage when Term Lenders (FIs) together hold
2/3 lending to the sick company resulting in FIs ignoring the views of W .C. Banks.

16
(vii) The time frame set for inquiry under section 424B is 60 days (extendable to 90 days) from
the date of receipt of the reference by the Tribunal. This includes the time of 21 days given to
the Operating Agency to submit its report. This is too short a period in cases involving large
organizations and complicated situations.
Government’s views in  seriatim
(i) Suggestion is accepted.
(ii) Remuneration etc. will be provided in the rules/regulations.
(iii) Labour is not owner but could be a creditor if wages due is not paid and they can
also submit a scheme under section 424A(11).
(iv) Accepted. The clause should be amended to state that erosion will be reported within
180 days of erosion of net worth coming to notice, or within 60 days of final
adoption of accounts, whichever is earlier. In case this amendment is agreed to
extension of 90 days may not be required.
(v) Regarding time limit, it is hoped that these can be adhered to, failing which NCLT
will have to record reasons in writing for not adhering thereto.
(vi) Accepted. As in amalgamation/demerger provisions, value will be taken as criteria.
(vii) Accepted. The clause should be amended to state that erosion will be reported within
180 days of erosion of net worth coming to notice, or within 60 days of final
adoption of accounts, whichever is earlier. In case this amendment is agreed to,
extension of 90 days may not be required. Regarding time limit, it is hoped that these
can be adhered to, failing which NCLT will have to record reasons in writing for
not adhering thereto.
Committee’s recommendation
16.12 The Committee recommends that suitable amendments may be made in the clause
so as to incorporate the suggestions as agreed to by the Government.
16.13 Subject to above the clause is adopted.
Clause 43
16.14 This clause seeks to insert a new section 439A in the Companies Act, 1956 relating to
statement of affairs to be filed on winding up of a company.
Suggestion
The details of all workers and employees and any amounts outstanding to or in respect of
them may be included in the statement of affairs to be filed.
Government’s views
This can be taken care of in the rules.
Committee’s recommendation
16.15 The Committee recommends that suitable provision may be made in the Rules.

17
16.16 Subject to above the clause is adopted.
Clause 45
16.17 This clause seeks to insert new sections in the principal Act for levy by way of cess and
formation of a Rehabilitation and Revival Fund.
Suggestions
(i) Section 44lA proposes to create a fund by imposing a levy on the annual turnover of every
company. The monitoring of collection by ROCs may prove to be cumbersome and difficult. In
order to ensure that the company have credited the amount of levy to the Central Government, the
statutory auditors may be directed to include a statement in their report. ROCs can examine the
report and recover the levy from the defaulting companies.
(ii) It should also be provided that once ‘cess funds’ are used for payment of workers dues
for rehabilitation or for security of assets, these would be recovered out of the sales proceeds of
the assets of the company or from the operations of the company during the implementation of
rehabilitation scheme.
(iii) Cess should be allowed as a deduction for purposes of income tax.
(iv) Write back of cess should be allowed for calculation of net profits, under section 349.
Expenses incurred out of the fund should be for the purposes of the fund mentioned in section
44lD and not for payment of salary/office expenses etc. of the staff members.
Government’s views in seriatim
(i) The suggestion may be considered at the time of framing of rules for this purpose.
(ii) This can be considered.
(iii) This can be considered.
(iv) This can be considered.
Committee’s recommendations
16.18 The Committee recommends that the suggestions as agreed to by the Government
may be effected and the clause suitably amended.
16.19 Subject to above, the clause is adopted.
Clause 51
16.20 This clause relates to appointment of official liquidator.
Suggestion
The clause proposes to substitute section 448 of the Principal Act which provides that the
Official Liquidator may be a body corporate consisting of such professionals as may be approved
by the Central Government from time to time. Since professionals are not allowed under the
respective statutes (Chartered Accountants Act, 1949, Cost and Works Accountants Act, 1959 and
Company Secretaries Act, 1980) governing them to form a body corporate. Thus, sub-clause (b)
containing the words ‘body corporate’ may be deleted.

18
Government’s views
Government agreed to delete sub-clause (b).
Committee’s recommendations
16.21 The Committee recommends deletion of sub-clause (b) as agreed to by the
Government.
16.22 Subject to above amendment, the clause is adopted.
Clause 77
16.23 This clause seeks to omit section 513 of the Principal Act which provides that a body
corporate shall not be appointed as liquidator. Clause 51 of the Bill proposes to substitute section
448 of that Act, inter alia, to provide that a body corporate can be appointed as official liquidator.
Government’s views
Since the relevant sub-clause in clause 51 is proposed to be deleted, this clause is not required.
Committee’s recommendation
16.24 The Committee recommends deletion of the clause.
Clause 118
16.25 This clause seeks to amend section 643 of the Principal Act.
Suggestion
Under section 643 (b) the Supreme Court has framed rules for winding up of a company
which had been implemented by the High Courts. Several of these powers cannot be exercised by
a presiding officer inferior to the courts. For example, the power of transfer under section 446
and the power to stay the proceedings in other courts, stand vested with High Court alone. It is
not possible to vest all these powers with the Tribunal which is a body inferior to the High Court.
Thus, the Tribunal at Chennai cannot be given the power to transfer proceedings pending before
the Bombay High Court.
Government’s views
This will be taken care of while framing the rules.
Committee’s recommendations:
16.26 The Committee recommends that the point raised may be considered while framing
the rules.
16.27 Subject to above, the clause is adopted.
Clause 1, Enacting Formula and the Title
16.28 Clause 1, the Enacting Formula and the title were adopted with some changes which
are of consequential nature, namely, ‘2001’ and ‘Fifty-second’ to be substituted by ‘2002’
and ‘Fifty-third’, respectively.

19
Suggestion not specific to clauses of the Bill
16.29 It has been pointed out that the word ‘Court’ appears in Sections 100, 101, 102 and 103
of the Companies Act, 1956. Consequent upon the enactment of the Bill, the word ‘Court’
wherever it occurs in the said sections needs to be substituted by the word ‘Tribunal’.
Government’s views
16.30 The Committee may consider the suggestion.
Committee’s recommendations
16.31 The Committee recommends that the amendments suggested in aforesaid sections of
the Companies Act, 1956 may be carried out.
16.32 The Committee recommends that the Bill be passed after incorporating the
amendments suggested by it and also those agreed to by the Government.
16.33 The Committee also recommends that the Government should give due consideration
to the fundamental issues raised during the deliberations in the Committee as enumerated
in paras 11.0 to 15.5.2 of the Report.

OBSERV ATIONS/CONCLUSIONS/RECOMMENDATIONS — AT A GLANCE
* Before the Committee could take up the Bill clause-by-clause, some fundamental
issues concerning the Bill were raised by members which are given below along with
the comments of the Ministry. (Para 10)
Rationality of creating NCLT
* A section of members in the Committee felt that instead of repealing SICA, 1985
and replacing BIFR with NCLT. BIFR should be provided with more teeth as cases
pending with it would be abated leading to serious socio-economic problems since
1306 cases relating to sick companies are pending at various stages in BIFR and
AAFIR involving 15 lakh employees/workers. Furthermore, the aim and objective of
the present Bill is different from that of SICA. In this context, the Committee
wanted to know from the Government the rationale behind creation of NCLT. The
Committee was not convinced with the Government’s explanation that BIFR/AAIFR
was not effective. (Para 11.0)
* The Committee noted the responses of the Ministry to the various issues raised.
However, a section of Members was not satisfied with the stand taken by the
Ministry and reiterated that status-quo in regard to Sick Industrial Companies Act
(SICA) may be maintained and BIFR/AAFIR may be strengthened by plugging the
loopholes. However, provisions may be made in the present Bill to transfer cases to
NCLT for winding up only from BIFR/AAIFR. (Para 11.3)
Rule Making Powers
* The Committee wondered as to who would make the rules for NCLT, as in the case
of High Courts, Supreme Court made the rules. (Para 12.0)
* The Committee while looking at the structure of the NCLT felt that it was becoming
overtly judicial. It was of the view that there should be representatives from all
walks of life in the Tribunal, viz. economics, law, labour interests etc. (Para 12.3)
* A point was raised about the constitutional validity of the Cess. An opinion
was expressed that if the Cess collected went to the Consolidated Fund of India
(CFI), it would be difficult to retrieve that money for rehabilitation of sick units.
It felt that it should be separated from it. The Department in its reply said that
the Bill had been thoroughly examined by the Legal and Legislative Departments.
The constitutional validity of the cess did not therefore, seem to be in doubt.
(Para 13.0)
* A point was raised that the levy of cess shall be upon all companies. It was suggested
that Section 441A (part VIA of the Bill) should restrict itself in its application to
“industrial companies” as defined under the section 2 (f) of the Bill for the following
reason:
The “Rehabilitation and Revival Fund” will not be available for the
rehabilitation or revival of Private Financial Institutions (PFIs), Banks, or
20

21
Service Sector. It, therefore, would not be equitable to expect contributions
from these bodies towards the fund since it had no application and therefore
no relevance, to them.
(Para 13.2)
* The Committee felt that there was a need to cover the Service Sector under the
Revival and Rehabilitation Scheme, because the Government was levying cess on
them. It felt that it would not be a fair deal, if they are asked to contribute to the
Revival and Rehabilitation Fund without incentives given to them. Institutionalising
early warning systems to avert sickness (Para 13.3)
* The Committee was strongly in favour of devising a mechanism to detect sickness
of industries at an early stage. (Para 14.0)
* The Committee felt that financial institutions/Banks were always in a better position
to detect sickness. Therefore, it should be made obligatory on them to inform the
NCLT, as and when they notice the sickness. (Para 14.3)
Timely disposal of cases
* A point was raised that in the BIFR, the number of days taken for sanction of a
scheme was 270 days, whereas in the case of the NCLT, it was 580 days. The
Committee wanted to know from the Department of Company Affairs as to how it
(the Department) was planning to expedite things when the time limit was more in
the new regime. (Para 15.0)
* Out of the 122 clauses in the Bill, the clauses discussed in the succeeding paras are
those wherein either the Committee or the Ministry has submitted/recommended
certain views/amendments. All other clauses including the Schedule have been
adopted by the Committee without any change. (Para 16.0)
Committee’s recommendation
* The Committee recommends that the clause may be suitably amended so as to
incorporate the suggestions accepted by the Government. (Para 16.2)
* Subject to above the clause is adopted.
(Para 16.3)
* The Committee is of the view that the Bill should provide for continuation of the
services of the Chairman, Vice-Chairman, Members and other employees in the
Tribunal for the following reasons:
(i) Unlike other bodies like BIFR/MRTP wherein members are appointed
on tenure basis, in the CLB members have been appointed on regular basis as
in the case of ITA T/CEGAT and as such their services should not be
terminated.
(ii) Unlike the matters pending before BIFR which would abate on establishment
of the Tribunal, the matters pending before the CLB would be transferred to
the Tribunal and therefore to ensure continuity, the services of the Members
should be protected. Therefore, the Government could not take the stand that
since the services of the Members of BIFR were sought to be terminated, the
same was applicable to the members of the CLB.

22
(iii) It is to be noted that in the cer tain Bills amending the Companies Act, 1956,
presented before the Parliament earlier, statutory protection had been provided
for the Members of the CLB. It was also to be noted that the Companies Bill,
1997, containing the provisions for statutory protection to the Members in
Clause 416 (2) had been cleared by the Committee.
(iv) Fur ther, the present Bill had no provisions for a Vice President in the NCLT.
All Tribunals in India which had more than one Regional bench had Vice
Presidents viz. ITAT, CEGAT, CAT, Settlement Commissions etc. Therefore, in
the NCLT also there should be atleast one Vice President.
(v) In view of the above, Clause 5 (2) should be amended on the following lines:
“Provided that the Central Government, by notification, continue the
appointment of the Chairman, Vice-Chairman or any other Member of the
Board of Company Law Administration, functioning as such before the
commencement of this Act, as President, Vice President or any other member
of the Tribunal/appellate Tribunal for such period not less than their existing
terms of office as may be specified in the notification.”
(vi) Most of the staff of the CLB are those who came on deputation and got
absorbed in the CLB. Since all the matters before CLB are to be transferred
to the Tribunal, all the staff of the CLB should also be taken on the Tribunal
and consequent provision should be made in the Bill.
(Para 16.5)
* The Committee recommends that suitable amendments may be made in clause 5 in
order to incorporate the above suggestions. (Para 16.6)
* Subject to the above observations and suggested amendments the clause is adopted.
Committee’s recommendation: (Para 16.7)
* The clause may be suitably amended to incorporate the suggestion accepted by the
Government. (Para 16.9)
* Subject to above, the clause is adopted.
(Para 16.10)
* The Committee recommends that suitable amendments may be made in the clause
so as to incorporate the suggestions as agreed to by the Government. (Para 16.12)
* Subject to above the clause is adopted.
(Para 16.13)
* The Committee recommends that the suggestions as agreed to by the Government
may be effected and the clause suitably amended. (Para 16.18)
* Subject to above, the clause is adopted.
(Para 16.19)
* The Committee recommends deletion of sub-clause (b) as agreed to by the
Government. (Para 16.21)
* Subject to above amendment, the clause is adopted.
(Para 16.22)
* The Committee recommends deletion of the clause.
(Para 16.24)

23
* The Committee recommends that the point raised may be considered while framing
the rules. (Para 16.26)
* Subject to above, the clause is adopted.
(Para 16.27)
Clause 1, Enacting Formula and the Title
* Clause 1, the Enacting Formula and the title were adopted with some changes which
are of consequential nature, namely, ‘2001’ and ‘Fifty-second’ to be substituted by
‘2002’ and ‘Fifty-third’, respectively. (Para 16.28)
* The Committee recommends that the amendments suggested in aforesaid sections of
the Companies Act, 1956 may be carried out. (Para 16.31)
* The Committee recommends that the Bill be passed after incorporating the
amendments suggested by it and also those agreed to by the Government.
(Para 16.32)
* The Committee also recommends that the Government should give due consideration
to the fundamental issues raised during the deliberations in the Committee as
enumerated in paras 11.0 to 15.5.2 of the Report. (Para 16.33)

WITNESSES
Representatives of Department of Company Affairs (Ministry of Law, Justice and
Company Affairs)
1. Shri V .K. Dhall, Secretary
2. Shri A. Ramaswami, Joint Secretary
3. Shri L.M. Gupta, Director
4. Shri S.B. Mathur, Consultant
5. Shri N.L. Vidhyanathan, Legal Advisor (NDDB)
6. Dr. N.V . Belavadi, GM (Co-op. Services, NDDB)
7. Shri S.P. Dixit, Joint Director (Legal)
8. Shri S.K. Sharma, Assistant Director
2. The Chairman welcomed the Secretary and other officers of Department of Company
Affairs. In his opening observations pointed out that the Committee was examining two Bills,
namely, the Companies (Amendment) Bill 2001 and the Companies (Second Amendment) Bill, 2001
bringing out comprehensive amendment in the Company Law. The Chairman observed that earlier
the Committee had examined the Companies Bill, 1997 and the Companies (Second Amendment)
Bill, 1999 through which the laws pertaining to the corporate governance had undergone a major
change but at that point of time the Government had informed the Committee that the issues
relating to the winding up, insolvency etc. were being examined by the Eradi Committee. The
Chairman further pointed out that with the availability of the report of that Committee the
Government had come forward with these two Bills.
3. Thereafter, the Chairman requested the Secretary to make detailed presentations on the Bills.
4. The Secretary made an audio-visual presentation on the various provisions of the Bills
highlighting salient features thereof.
5. The Secretary stated that the Companies (Amendment) Bill, 2001 was related to the
insolvency proceedings whereas the Companies (Second Amendment) Bill, 2001 related to the
registration of Cooperatives as producer companies.
6. The Secretary further elaborated why a separate insolvency law was needed as per
recommendations of the Eradi Committee. He was of the view that Board of Industrial and
Financial Reconstruction (BIFR) was being misused by the managements of the sick companies and
more over it could not succeed to the test of time. He informed that through the Companies
(Amendment) Bill, 2001, it had been proposed to dissolve Companies Law Board (CLB) and BIFR
and establish National Company Law Tribunal and the National Company Law Appellate Tribunal
in place thereof. The Secretary also informed that the recommendations of the Committee on Home
Affairs contained in its 65th Report regarding appointment of professionals from cross section of
the society to the Tribunals and official liquidator had been taken care of in the Bill.
7. The Secretary, further, stated that the Bill provide for the Rehabilitation and Revival Fund
as recommended by the Eradi Committee. The Bills also contained the scheme of time bound
disposal of revival, rehabilitation and winding up of companies.
8. After the Secretary’s presentation, the Chairman wanted to know the methodology used in
computation of days for assessing the erosion of company’s net assets, as the erosion did not take
place overnight; for revival schemes, especially in regard to section 424 B & C. Then the Chairman
requested the Members to seek clarifications.

29
9. The Members raised a number of points/suggestions which are summarised below:
(i) Drafting of clause 10 FR was objected to as it stipulated that the Chairman. NCLT
would be a judge of the High Court. It was contended that some of the officers of
the Company Law Board were highly experienced and learned who should be given
chance in the NCLT.
(ii) Chairman of NCLT should necessarily be a judge of the High Court.
(iii) The employees’ of the Tribunal should be protected.
(iv) There should a social obligation clause in this legislation.
10. The Chairman requested the Secretary to send the responses to the queries of the Members
within 7-10 days and make his presentation on the Companies (Second Amendment) 2001, which
was based on the recommendations of Alagh Committee.

of the Bill. He suggested some points to be considered in respect of definitions of sick companies.
The FlCCl’s apprehension was about the National Company Law Tribunal (NCLT) which was being
entrusted with multifarious functions, as a result of which, it might go ultimately the BIFR way.
The CII suggested that NCLT should have the competent members having experience and
qualification required for handling complex issues of corporate affairs. They were afraid that the
NCLT should not be made the dumping ground of retired/retiring bureaucrats. FICCI emphasized
the need for giving more teeth to the NCLT.

on the various provisions of the Bill.

Companies (Amendment) Bill, 2001. He also suggested for the effective administration of justice in
respect of corporate governance by NCLT and hoped that the deficiency of the Company Law
Board will not be repeated by the proposed high powered tribunal particularly when the entire
administration relating to the rehabilitation, revival, winding up, companies mergers/amalgamations,
oppression, mismanagement and all other petitions relating to reduction of capital were being taken
away from the court and handed over to the tribunal. The representative, then, expressed the
Chambers view on the Revival Fund.
8. The PHD  Chambers of Commerce also suggested for amendment in the Advocate Act, the
Company Secretary Act, the Chartered Accountant’s Act etc. for registering a law firm as a
company.
9. The members of the Committee asked clarifications on some of the points arising out of
the presentation of PHD Chambers of Commerce.

6. The Chairman welcomed the Chairman and Officers of the Board of Industrial and Financial
Reconstruction (BIFR) and referred to certain issues related to the Companies (Amendment)
Bill, 2001 e.g. proposed repeal of Sick Industrial Companies (Special Provisions) Act, 1985 (SICA),
winding up of Company Law Board, insolvency etc. He then requested the Chairman, BIFR to
express viewpoints of the Board on the legislation.
7. The Chairman, BIFR stated that the two inter-connected Bills viz. the Sick Industrial
(Special Provisions) Repeal Bill, 2001 were introduced in the Parliament, out of which former was
pending with the Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Finance while later was
referred to this Committee. The Chairman of the Committee was of the opinion that the discussion
should confine to the Companies (Amendment) Bill, 2001 and the Companies (Amendment)
Bill, 2001 only because the former Bill had been referred to the other Committee.
8. The Chairman, BIFR expressed the views/points of his organization on the Bill in detail. He
also expressed his views on the proposed establisbment of National Company Law Tribunal and
National Company Law Appellate Tribunal.
9. The Members thereafter put forward certain queries, some of which were replied to and
for the rest, BIFR was asked to send written responses.
(The Witnesses then withdrew)

Association (IBA) and requested them express their views/suggestion on the Bill.
11. The Chairman, IBA stated that due to huge pendency of cases before BIFR, it became inevitable
to replace it with another efficient organization i.e. NCLT because as a sequel to sick companies,
the banks were also facing problems in the form of non-performing assets. He also suggested for

2. At the outset Chairman welcomed Members and announced the reference of three Bills to
the Committee for consideration and report, namely, Election and Other Related Laws (Amendment)
Bill, 2002, Repatriation of Prisoners Bill, 2002 and Supreme Court Judges (Salaries and Conditions
of Service) Amendment Bill, 2002. He also informed Members of the allocation of the Department
of Development of North Eastern Region to the Committee.
2.1 He then announced the schedule of meetings of the Committee during the current
inter-session period to consider further subjects and Bills pending its consideration.
3. Chairman then welcomed the representative of Madras Bar Association and made general
observations on the features of the Companies (Amendment) Bill, 2001. He then requested the
witness to make his presentation on the Bill.
4. The witness, while thanking the Chairman and Members of the Committee objected to the
formation of a Tribunal for the purpose of taking over all the powers and functions of the Board
of Industrial and Financial Reconstruction (BIFR), the Company Law Board (CLB) and the High
Courts. He stressed that instead of repealing the SICA, Section 22 of the Act can be watered down
and BIFR may be allowed to continue and CLB could be given a different form as proposed in
the 1997 Bill. He however, supported the privatisation of the Office of the Liquidator.
4.1 The witness also questioned the feasibility of appointing 62 members of NCLT when it was
not possible to appoint even nine members of CLB.
5. At this point, Chairman wanted to know the views of the Madras Bar Association as
to whether the Bill would be able to achieve the objectives of rehabilitation, reconstruction
and revival of sick companies. Members also raised a number of queries arising out of the
presentation and requested that a detailed elucidated analysis of the working of the institutions
which are being considered for abolition/merger with other institutions, may be furnished to the
Committee.

Law Tribunal, constitution of the Appallate Tribunal, Auditors’ Report on Sickness, powers of the
Tribunal to make suitable order, misfeasance proceedings, statement of affairs, rehabilitation and
